What brings you in to see us today?\n\nPatient: Everyone keeps telling me I’m too thin and need to gain weight, but I honestly don’t see what the big problem is with my size right now. My family and my primary doctor are the ones who really pushed me to come in and get evaluated.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal or is there a possibility that you are underweight, even if you don't see it as a problem?\n\nPatient: My doctor and my sister keep telling me I'm underweight and look \"frail,\" but I really feel like they are overreacting. What brings you in to see us today?\n\nPatient: Everyone keeps telling me I’m too thin and need to gain weight, but I honestly don’t see what the big problem is with my size right now. My family and my primary doctor are the ones who really pushed me to come in and get evaluated.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal or is there a possibility that you are underweight, even if you don't see it as a problem?\n\nPatient: My doctor and my sister keep telling me I'm underweight and look \"frail,\" but I really feel like they are overreacting. When I look in the mirror, I just see myself as the lean, wiry kind of strong I've always wanted to be. I don't see my weight as a problem at all, and I don't understand why everyone thinks it is so dangerous.\n\nDoctor: Do you restrict your food intake or engage in excessive exercise to maintain your current weight, and if so, is this behavior intended to avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Most days I only have coffee in the morning, maybe an apple or some nuts mid-day, and a little bowl of soup at night, sometimes just moving food around my plate so my daughter thinks I've eaten. I do this because if I even think about adding rice or bread back in, I get a tight, panicky feeling in my chest and worry about bloating or gaining weight overnight. I would honestly rather go to bed a little hungry than face that risk.\n\nDoctor: Do you also engage in excessive exercise, such as intense workouts or excessive physical activity, in addition to restricting your food intake, to maintain your current weight?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't call it excessive or intense at all. I immediately picture my stomach puffing out and my face getting bloated overnight, which terrifies me. Going to bed a little hungry feels so much safer than risking that kind of change.\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself in the mirror, do you see your actual body shape and weight, or do you see a different image, perhaps one that is heavier or thinner than you really are?\n\nPatient: When I look in the mirror, I see myself as lean, wiry, and strong—exactly how I want to look to stay agile in my carpentry work. To me, it is a healthy, sharp image, so I honestly don't understand why others look at me and see someone \"frail\" or \"too thin.\" I feel like I am seeing my actual body clearly, and it's exactly where I want it to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your weight is currently less than 85% of the normal weight for someone of your age, sex, and height, or do you think it's closer to a normal weight?\n\nPatient: No, I don't believe my weight is dangerously low or anywhere below 85% of what is normal. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see us today?\n\nPatient: Everyone keeps telling me I’m too thin and need to gain weight, but I honestly don’t see what the big problem is with my size right now. My family and my primary doctor are the ones who really pushed me to come in and get evaluated.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal or is there a possibility that you are underweight, even if you don't see it as a problem?\n\nPatient: My doctor and my sister keep telling me I'm underweight and look \"frail,\" but I really feel like they are overreacting. When I look in the mirror, I just see myself as the lean, wiry kind of strong I've always wanted to be. I don't see my weight as a problem at all, and I don't understand why everyone thinks it is so dangerous.\n\nDoctor: Do you restrict your food intake or engage in excessive exercise to maintain your current weight, and if so, is this behavior intended to avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Most days I only have coffee in the morning, maybe an apple or some nuts mid-day, and a little bowl of soup at night, sometimes just moving food around my plate so my daughter thinks I've eaten. I do this because if I even think about adding rice or bread back in, I get a tight, panicky feeling in my chest and worry about bloating or gaining weight overnight. I would honestly rather go to bed a little hungry than face that risk.\n\nDoctor: Do you also engage in excessive exercise, such as intense workouts or excessive physical activity, in addition to restricting your food intake, to maintain your current weight?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't call it excessive or intense at all. I just do some tai chi and cycling to stay active, agile, and youthful as I get older. It's really just a normal, healthy part of my daily routine to keep my joints moving, not some extreme workout regimen.\n\nDoctor: When you think about gaining weight, what is it specifically about being heavier that scares or bothers you the most?\n\nPatient: If I even think about eating heavier foods like rice or bread, I get this tight, panicky feeling in my chest. I immediately picture my stomach puffing out and my face getting bloated overnight, which terrifies me. Going to bed a little hungry feels so much safer than risking that kind of change.\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself in the mirror, do you see your actual body shape and weight, or do you see a different image, perhaps one that is heavier or thinner than you really are?\n\nPatient: When I look in the mirror, I see myself as lean, wiry, and strong—exactly how I want to look to stay agile in my carpentry work. To me, it is a healthy, sharp image, so I honestly don't understand why others look at me and see someone \"frail\" or \"too thin.\" I feel like I am seeing my actual body clearly, and it's exactly where I want it to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your weight is currently less than 85% of the normal weight for someone of your age, sex, and height, or do you think it's closer to a normal weight?\n\nPatient: No, I don't believe my weight is dangerously low or anywhere below 85% of what is normal. I honestly think my doctor and family are overreacting, because to me, I just look lean, wiry, and strong. I feel like my weight is perfectly fine for my age and what I do.\n\nDoctor: When you say you see yourself as 'lean, wiry, and strong,' do you think that's how others see you as well, or is there a difference in how you perceive your body versus how others perceive it?\n\nPatient: There is definitely a big difference in how we see it. While I look in the mirror and see the lean, wiry, and strong person I’ve always wanted to be, my sister calls me \"frail\" and my doctor is constantly worrying about my weight. I really feel like they are overreacting, and I don't understand why they see my size as such a dangerous problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight and your perception of your body shape are related to your self-worth or are they more about maintaining a certain physical appearance?"

What brings you in to see us today?\n\nPatient: Everyone keeps telling me I’m too thin and need to gain weight, but I honestly don’t see what the big problem is with my size right now. My family and my primary doctor are the ones who really pushed me to come in and get evaluated.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal or is there a possibility that you are underweight, even if you don't see it as a problem?\n\nPatient: My doctor and my sister keep telling me I'm underweight and look \"frail,\" but I really feel like they are overreacting. I don't see my weight as a problem at all, and I don't understand why everyone thinks it is so dangerous.\n\nDoctor: Do you restrict your food intake or engage in excessive exercise to maintain your current weight, and if so, is this behavior intended to avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Most days I only have coffee in the morning, maybe an apple or some nuts mid-day, and a little bowl of soup at night, sometimes just moving food around my plate so my daughter thinks I've eaten. I do this because if I even think about adding rice or bread back in, I get a tight, panicky feeling in my chest and worry about bloating or gaining weight overnight. I would honestly rather go to bed a little hungry than face that risk.\n\nDoctor: Do you also engage in excessive exercise, such as intense workouts or excessive physical activity, in addition to restricting your food intake, to maintain your current weight?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't call it excessive or intense at all. I honestly think my doctor and family are overreacting, because to me, I just look lean, wiry, and strong. I feel like my weight is perfectly fine for my age and what I do.\n\nDoctor: When you say you see yourself as 'lean, wiry, and strong,' do you think that's how others see you as well, or is there a difference in how you perceive your body versus how others perceive it?\n\nPatient: There is definitely a big difference in how we see it. I really feel like they are overreacting, and I don't understand why they see my size as such a dangerous problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight and your perception of your body shape are related to your self-worth or are they more about maintaining a certain physical appearance?\n\nPatient: It is deeply tied to both, but especially to my sense of worth and identity as an artisan. Maintaining this lean, wiry frame makes me feel sharp, youthful, and capable in my carpentry work as I face aging and transitions in my career. It feels like a quiet victory that proves I am still disciplined and meticulous, rather than just a matter of superficial looks.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you think would happen to your sense of self-worth and identity as an artisan if you were to gain a small amount of weight?"

I do this because if I even think about adding rice or bread back in, I get a tight, panicky feeling in my chest and worry about bloating or gaining weight overnight. I would honestly rather go to bed a little hungry than face that risk.\n\nDoctor: Do you also engage in excessive exercise, such as intense workouts or excessive physical activity, in addition to restricting your food intake, to maintain your current weight?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't call it excessive or intense at all. I just do some tai chi and cycling to stay active, agile, and youthful as I get older. It's really just a normal, healthy part of my daily routine to keep my joints moving, not some extreme workout regimen.\n\nDoctor: When you think about gaining weight, what is it specifically about being heavier that scares or bothers you the most?\n\nPatient: If I even think about eating heavier foods like rice or bread, I get this tight, panicky feeling in my chest. I immediately picture my stomach puffing out and my face getting bloated overnight, which terrifies me. Going to bed a little hungry feels so much safer than risking that kind of change.\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself in the mirror, do you see your actual body shape and weight, or do you see a different image, perhaps one that is heavier or thinner than you really are?\n\nPatient: When I look in the mirror, I see myself as lean, wiry, and strong—exactly how I want to look to stay agile in my carpentry work. To me, it is a healthy, sharp image, so I honestly don't understand why others look at me and see someone \"frail\" or \"too thin.\" I feel like I am seeing my actual body clearly, and it's exactly where I want it to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your weight is currently less than 85% of the normal weight for someone of your age, sex, and height, or do you think it's closer to a normal weight?\n\nPatient: No, I don't believe my weight is dangerously low or anywhere below 85% of what is normal. I honestly think my doctor and family are overreacting, because to me, I just look lean, wiry, and strong. I feel like my weight is perfectly fine for my age and what I do.\n\nDoctor: When you say you see yourself as 'lean, wiry, and strong,' do you think that's how others see you as well, or is there a difference in how you perceive your body versus how others perceive it?\n\nPatient: There is definitely a big difference in how we see it. While I look in the mirror and see the lean, wiry, and strong person I’ve always wanted to be, my sister calls me \"frail\" and my doctor is constantly worrying about my weight. I really feel like they are overreacting, and I don't understand why they see my size as such a dangerous problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight and your perception of your body shape are related to your self-worth or are they more about maintaining a certain physical appearance?\n\nPatient: It is deeply tied to both, but especially to my sense of worth and identity as an artisan. Maintaining this lean, wiry frame makes me feel sharp, youthful, and capable in my carpentry work as I face aging and transitions in my career. It feels like a quiet victory that proves I am still disciplined and meticulous, rather than just a matter of superficial looks.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you think would happen to your sense of self-worth and identity as an artisan if you were to gain a small amount of weight?\n\nPatient: If I were to gain weight, I feel like I would lose my sharp edge and start slowing down, which feels like giving in to aging. It would threaten my identity as a disciplined artisan because I’d no longer feel like the agile, capable person who can keep up on a demanding job site. To me, gaining weight feels like admitting defeat and losing the control I've worked so hard to maintain.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your fear of gaining weight is primarily driven by a fear of losing control over your body and your artisan identity, or is it more about a genuine fear of the health consequences of being overweight?"
  }
]

It is absolutely about maintaining control over my aging body and protecting my identity as an artisan, not about the health consequences of being overweight. Being light and lean makes me feel sharp, agile, and capable on the job site, which fits my reputation as a meticulous craftsman. Gaining weight just feels like losing that sharp edge and becoming slow or puffy.
